Implicit in the design: the `ft_move > 0` mask is treated as the paper's curation step, so\nstalled periods are removed frame-by-frame instead of by discarding trials. The agent reported\n(step 42) \"the conversion ... has retained the intended full trial set—38,110 trials total are\nexpected—while filtering only non-running frames and neurons outside the four paper-defined\nvisual-area groups.\" No justification is given anywhere for keeping trials whose retained frames\nstill straddle very long pauses (16% of trials contain a within-trial gap > 5 s, 1.3% a gap > 30 s,\nthe worst spanning 1,765 s).\n\n## 2-a. What variables in the raw data is the `neural` data derived from?\n\ni. `spks` from `spk/<session_id>_neural_data.npy` — a list of `(n_neurons, n_frames)` float32\ndeconvolved traces, one per imaging plane. The area label of each neuron comes from `iarea` in\n`retinotopy/<mouse>_<date>_trans.npz`, whose length is asserted to equal the total neuron count\nacross planes.\n\nii.\n```python\n        raw_planes = np.load(spk_path, allow_pickle=True).item()[\"spks\"]\n        nframes = min(plane.shape[1] for plane in raw_planes)\n        if any(plane.dtype != np.float32 for plane in raw_planes):\n            raise TypeError(f\"Expected float32 deconvolved traces in {spk_path}\")\n        iarea = np.load(data_root / \"retinotopy\" / retino_name)[\"iarea\"]\n        raw_neuron_count = sum(plane.shape[0] for plane in raw_planes)\n        if len(iarea) != raw_neuron_count:\n            raise RuntimeError(...)\n```\n\niii. Module docstring: \"The paper's neural arrays are Suite2p non-negative deconvolved fluorescence\ntraces.\" Metadata records `\"neural_signal\": \"Suite2p non-negative deconvolved fluorescence\"`.\n\n## 2-b. How is the `neural` data processed?\n\ni. No transformation at all: no dF/F, no smoothing, no normalization, no spatial interpolation, no\nz-scoring. The retained neurons and the retained frames are selected plane by plane (to avoid\nmaterializing the whole session), the planes are concatenated in file order, and each trial's\ncolumns are copied into a contiguous `(n_neurons, n_timepoints)` float32 array. Values are kept in\nthe source dtype (float32), giving a 142 GB pickle.\n\nii.\n```python\n        selected_planes = []\n        offset = 0\n        for plane in raw_planes:\n            local_keep = np.flatnonzero(keep_neuron[offset : offset + plane.shape[0]])\n            selected_planes.append(plane[np.ix_(local_keep, frame_indices)])\n            offset += plane.shape[0]\n        selected_neural = np.concatenate(selected_planes, axis=0)\n```\n```python\n            trial_neural = np.ascontiguousarray(selected_neural[:, columns])\n```\n\niii. Module docstring: \"No additional smoothing, normalization, or spatial interpolation is applied\nhere: spatial interpolation in the paper was for position-aligned analyses, whereas the requested\ndecoder alignment is temporal (corridor entry).\" Step 30: \"It keeps the paper's four retinotopic\nvisual-area groups, removes atlas-unassigned neurons ... the resulting pickle is expected to be\nroughly 150 GB\" — the size was anticipated and accepted rather than reduced by down-casting.\n\n## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. The only neuron-level filter is the retinotopic area: a neuron is kept iff its `iarea` code is in\nV1 `{8}`, medial `{0,1,2,9}`, anterior `{3,4}` or lateral `{5,6}`. Codes `-1` (outside the atlas) and\n`7` are dropped. This keeps 4,105,393 of 4,691,034 neurons (V1 1,833,035; medial 1,108,860;\nanterior 668,180; lateral 495,318). No further selection (no activity threshold, no SNR cut) is\napplied. Frame-level curation is the running/corridor mask of 1-d.\n\nii.\n```python\nAREA_CODES = {\"V1\": (8,), \"medial\": (0, 1, 2, 9), \"anterior\": (3, 4), \"lateral\": (5, 6)}\n\ndef area_labels(iarea):\n    region = np.full(len(iarea), -1, dtype=np.int8)\n    for idx, name in enumerate(list(AREA_CODES)):\n        region[np.isin(iarea, AREA_CODES[name])] = idx\n    keep = region >= 0\n    return keep, region[keep].astype(np.int64)\n```\n\niii. Code comment: \"Exact grouping used by `code/utils.py::neu_area_ID`. Area -1 (outside the\nretinotopic atlas) and area 7 (not assigned to one of the four visual-area groups used in the paper)\nare excluded.\" Metadata repeats this. The agent checked the `iarea` histograms of several sessions\nin step 20 before fixing the grouping. Suite2p's own cell classifier had already curated the ROIs,\nso nothing further is done.\n\n## 2-d. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n\ni. Alignment is to corridor entry: the first column of a trial is the first retained frame after the\nmouse entered the 4 m textured corridor, and the last is the last retained frame before it left.\nTrials keep their natural, variable length; nothing is padded or truncated to a common window.\n`off_start = 0.0` and `off_end = None`. Because the mask also removes non-running frames, the\ncolumns of a trial are the *running* frames of that traversal and can be separated by arbitrarily\nlong real-time gaps (median max within-trial gap 0.63 s, but 16% of trials contain a gap > 5 s and\nthe largest is 1,765 s). The real elapsed time of each column is nevertheless supplied to the decoder\nas the `time_since_trial_start_s` input.\n\nii.\n```python\n            columns = np.flatnonzero(trial_ids == trial)\n            frames = frame_indices[columns]\n            times = beh[\"ft\"][frames]\n            trial_neural = np.ascontiguousarray(selected_neural[:, columns])\n```\n```python\n            \"temporal_alignment_event\": \"entry into the 4 m textured corridor\",\n            \"off_start\": 0.0,\n            \"off_end\": None,\n```\n\niii. Step 25 (quoted above) is the only explicit justification: the original imaging frames are\npreserved rather than resampled, and the agent notes that \"timestamps will therefore retain any gaps\ncaused by pauses\". The metadata field `\"frame_filter\": \"ft_CorrSpc & (ft_move > 0) with a valid\ntrial index\"` documents the consequence.\n\n## 2-e. What is the temporal resolution (time bin size) of the converted data? Is any temporal rebinning applied?\n\ni. No rebinning or resampling. One column per two-photon imaging frame; the nominal rate of 3.17 Hz\nis recorded as `time_bin_size = 1000/3.17 = 315.46 ms`, identical for every trial and session. (The\ntrue inter-frame interval varies slightly, 0.26–0.45 s, and dropped frames mean the columns are not\nuniformly spaced in time.)\n\nii.\n```python\nFRAME_RATE_HZ = 3.17\n...\n            \"time_bin_size\": 1000.0 / FRAME_RATE_HZ,\n            \"nominal_frame_rate_hz\": FRAME_RATE_HZ,\n```\n\niii. Not argued explicitly beyond the docstring's statement that no interpolation/resampling is\napplied because the requested alignment is temporal rather than positional. The imaging frame is the\nfinest resolution available and every behavioral stream (`ft_*`) is already on that same grid.\n\n## 3-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Time to sound cue* derived from?\n\ni. `SoundTime` (per-trial MATLAB datenum timestamp of the cue) and `ft` (datenum timestamp of every\nimaging frame). The frame-number variant `SoundFr` is not used.\n\nii.\n```python\n            times = beh[\"ft\"][frames]\n            time_to_sound = ((beh[\"SoundTime\"][trial] - times) * SECONDS_PER_DAY).astype(np.float32)\n```\n\niii. The agent compared the two representations in steps 21–22, printing\n`(ft[StartFr] - Trial_start_time)*86400` and the equivalent for the grey-space event and finding\nagreement to ~0.25 s (sub-frame). Using the recorded event timestamps directly avoids interpolating\na fractional frame number onto the time axis.\n\n## 3-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Time to sound cue*?\n\ni. `(SoundTime[trial] - ft[frame]) * 86400`, i.e. MATLAB datenum days converted to seconds, stored as\nfloat32 and evaluated for every retained frame of the trial. It is positive before the cue and\nnegative after it, matching the name \"time **to** sound cue\". Observed range over the dataset:\n−1763.3 s to +723.5 s (the extremes come from the trials with long stalls described in 1-e).\n\nii.\n```python\nSECONDS_PER_DAY = 86_400.0\n...\n            trial_input[0] = time_to_sound\n...\n            \"time_to_sound_sign\": \"positive before the cue; negative after the cue\",\n```\n\niii. The sign convention is documented explicitly in the metadata. No smoothing or clipping is\napplied; the agent did not comment on the extreme values.\n\n## 3-c. How is the `input` *Time to sound cue* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. It is evaluated on exactly the frames that supply the neural columns of that trial (`frames`\nderives from the same `columns` used to slice `selected_neural`), so it is element-wise aligned and\nhas the trial's length.\n\nii.\n```python\n            columns = np.flatnonzero(trial_ids == trial)\n            frames = frame_indices[columns]\n            times = beh[\"ft\"][frames]\n            trial_neural = np.ascontiguousarray(selected_neural[:, columns])\n            time_to_sound = ((beh[\"SoundTime\"][trial] - times) * SECONDS_PER_DAY)\n```\n\niii. All streams in this dataset (`ft`, `ft_*`) share the imaging-frame grid, so indexing every\nstream with the same frame list guarantees alignment.\n\n## 4-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Day of training* derived from?\n\ni. `datexp` (the recording date string) and `mname`, taken from the index entry of each session. The\n`days` field that exists on some `Imaging_Exp_info.npy` entries is deliberately *not* used.\n\nii.\n```python\n    for sid in session_ids:\n        record = sources[sid][\"record\"]\n        date = datetime.strptime(record[\"datexp\"], \"%Y_%m_%d\").date()\n        subject = str(record[\"mname\"])\n        parsed[sid] = (subject, date)\n        first_date[subject] = min(date, first_date.get(subject, date))\n```\n\niii. Docstring of `elapsed_days_by_session`: \"The experiment table does not provide a complete\ntraining-day counter (only selected later recordings have a `days` field). Dates are available for\nevery recording, so elapsed calendar day is the only uniform, non-imputed continuous measure across\nsupervised, unsupervised, grating, and naive mice.\" The agent verified this in step 47–48 by\nprinting `(exptype, sess#, days, stimtype, ...)` for all 89 recordings and seeing `days` populated\nonly for the `*_train2_after_learning` entries.\n\n## 4-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Day of training*?\n\ni. For each mouse, the earliest imaging date is day 0, and every session is the integer number of\ncalendar days elapsed since then, as a float. The value is per-session and is broadcast across all\nbins of all its trials. Observed range 0–92 days.\n\nii.\n```python\n    return {sid: float((date - first_date[subject]).days)\n            for sid, (subject, date) in parsed.items()}\n...\n            trial_input[1] = day_by_session[sid]\n...\n            \"training_day_definition\": \"elapsed calendar days since the subject's earliest imaging session\",\n```\n\niii. As above: calendar date is the only field available for every recording. The definition is\nwritten into the metadata so a downstream user knows it is elapsed calendar days, not a count of\ntraining sessions or of the mouse's true training day (training began ~2 weeks before the first\nrecording, per `methods.txt`).\n\n## 5-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Time since trial start* derived from?\n\ni. `Trial_start_time` (per-trial datenum of corridor entry) and `ft` (per-frame datenum). The frame\nvariant `StartFr` is not used.\n\nii.\n```python\n            time_from_start = ((times - beh[\"Trial_start_time\"][trial]) * SECONDS_PER_DAY).astype(np.float32)\n```\n\niii. Same as 3-a: the agent checked in step 22 that `ft[StartFr] - Trial_start_time` is within a\nquarter of a frame, so the recorded timestamp and the frame-index representation agree; the\ntimestamp is used directly.\n\n## 5-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Time since trial start*?\n\ni. `(ft[frame] - Trial_start_time[trial]) * 86400`, in seconds, float32, per frame. It is positive\nafter corridor entry (the first retained frame of a trial is always after entry, so the minimum over\nthe dataset is +6.0e-5 s). Maximum 1765.2 s, again from stalled trials.\n\nii.\n```python\n            trial_input[2] = time_from_start\n```\n\niii. No justification beyond the unit conversion; it is a direct time difference against the trial's\nalignment event.\n\n## 5-c. How is the `input` *Time since trial start* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Computed from `times = beh[\"ft\"][frames]` for the same `frames`/`columns` that produce the neural\nmatrix, so it is element-wise aligned and of identical length. Because non-running frames are\ndropped, consecutive elements are not equally spaced; this input is what tells the decoder the real\nelapsed time of each column.\n\nii.\n```python\n            frames = frame_indices[columns]\n            times = beh[\"ft\"][frames]\n            trial_neural = np.ascontiguousarray(selected_neural[:, columns])\n            trial_input[2] = ((times - beh[\"Trial_start_time\"][trial]) * SECONDS_PER_DAY)\n```\n\niii. Frame-indexed alignment for every stream, as in 3-c.\n\n## 6-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Reward availability* derived from?\n\ni. `isRew`, the per-trial boolean flag marking trials run in the rewarded corridor.\n\nii.\n```python\n            trial_input[3] = float(beh[\"isRew\"][trial])\n```\n\niii. Not discussed; `isRew` is a direct read of the required quantity. (The agent also carried\n`Reward_Mode` per session into `session_info` as context.)\n\n## 6-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Reward availability*?\n\ni. Cast from bool to float (0.0/1.0) and broadcast across every bin of the trial, as one row of the\n`(4, n_timepoints)` float32 input matrix. No other processing. Observed range 0–1.\n\nii.\n```python\n            trial_input = np.empty((4, ntime), dtype=np.float32)\n            ...\n            trial_input[3] = float(beh[\"isRew\"][trial])\n```\n\niii. None given; none needed.\n\n## 7-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Visual stimulus category* derived from?\n\ni. `WallName`, the per-trial name of the wall texture. (`WallType`, `ft_WallID`, `UniqWalls` and\n`stim_id` are not used; `stim_id` is masked in the swap sessions.)\n\nii.\n```python\ndef visual_vocabulary(behaviors: dict) -> list[str]:\n    return sorted({str(wall) for beh in behaviors.values() for wall in beh[\"WallName\"]})\n...\n            trial_output[0] = stimulus_to_id[str(beh[\"WallName\"][trial])]\n```\n\niii. Not argued explicitly. The agent counted the wall names over the whole dataset in step 53–54:\n`leaf1 9736, circle1 9393, leaf2 4841, rock1 2743, wood1 2517, circle2 1836, wood2 1592, leaf3 1545,\nleaf1_swap2 821, leaf1_swap1 818, wood5 567, rock2 535, wood1_swap2 421, circle3 390, wood1_swap1\n355` — 15 distinct names — and took the vocabulary from that count.\n\n## 7-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Visual stimulus category*?\n\ni. The 15 distinct `WallName` strings found across the whole dataset are sorted and used **as-is** as\nthe 15 category labels; no collapsing to the four base textures (circle / leaf / rock / wood) is\nperformed, so `circle1`, `circle2`, `circle3` are three separate classes, and `leaf1`,\n`leaf1_swap1`, `leaf1_swap2` are three more. The per-trial index into that vocabulary is broadcast\nover all bins of the trial. Resulting class frequencies range from 0.8% to 26.4% of bins.\n\nii.\n```python\n    stimulus_values = visual_vocabulary(behaviors)\n    stimulus_to_id = {name: idx for idx, name in enumerate(stimulus_values)}\n...\n            trial_output[0] = stimulus_to_id[str(beh[\"WallName\"][trial])]\n...\n        \"output_values\": [stimulus_values, ...]\n```\n\niii. No justification is recorded in the code comments, the metadata, or the trajectory for the\nchoice of granularity; the agent simply adopted the raw `WallName` vocabulary it had enumerated.\n\n## 8-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Licking* derived from?\n\ni. `LickFr`, the (fractional) imaging-frame number of every lick in the session.\n\nii.\n```python\n        lick_frame = beh[\"LickFr\"]\n        lick_frame = lick_frame[np.isfinite(lick_frame)].astype(np.int64)\n```\n\niii. Not discussed; `LickFr` is already expressed on the imaging-frame grid, so it is the natural\nsource.\n\n## 8-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Licking*?\n\ni. Non-finite lick entries are dropped, the remaining fractional frame numbers are truncated to the\nframe they fall in, and a bin is labelled 1 if any lick falls in it, 0 otherwise — a binary\ntime series, `output_values = [\"not licking\", \"licking\"]`. Licks that fall on frames removed by the\nrunning/corridor mask are simply never matched. Resulting positive rate 3.69% of bins.\n\nii.\n```python\n            trial_output[1] = np.isin(frames, lick_frame).astype(np.int64)\n```\n\niii. Not discussed. (Note that the `ft_move > 0` mask removes exactly the periods in which task mice\nstopped to collect reward, which `methods.txt` describes as the paper's intent, and which removes\nsome licking frames.)\n\n## 8-c. How is `output` *Licking* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. `np.isin(frames, lick_frame)` is evaluated on the same absolute frame indices that select the\nneural columns, so the lick flag is element-wise aligned with the neural matrix and has the trial's\nlength.\n\nii.\n```python\n            frames = frame_indices[columns]\n            trial_neural = np.ascontiguousarray(selected_neural[:, columns])\n            trial_output[1] = np.isin(frames, lick_frame).astype(np.int64)\n```\n\niii. Frame-indexed alignment for every stream, as in 3-c.\n\n## 9-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Position in corridor* derived from?\n\ni. `ft_Pos`, the position inside the corridor at each imaging frame, in decimeters, together with the\nper-session `Texture_Length` field (40 dm in every session) used to set the bin width.\n\nii.\n```python\n    out[\"texture_length_dm\"] = float(beh[\"Texture_Length\"])\n...\n            position_bin = np.floor(beh[\"ft_Pos\"][frames] / (beh[\"texture_length_dm\"] / 4.0)).astype(np.int64)\n```\n\niii. Code comment: \"Texture_Length is 40 decimeters. Using the recorded setting keeps the four bins\nexactly 1 m even if a future source file differs.\" Metadata records\n`\"position_source_units\": \"decimeters\"`. The agent verified in step 22 that within the mask `ft_Pos`\nranges 0.0004–39.998 dm.\n\n## 9-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Position in corridor*?\n\ni. Position in decimeters is divided by `Texture_Length/4 = 10` dm and floored, giving an integer\n0–3; the value is computed per frame (time-varying) and stored as one row of the int64 output\nmatrix. Resulting bin occupancies 25.00 / 24.87 / 24.96 / 25.17%.\n\nii.\n```python\n            position_bin = np.floor(beh[\"ft_Pos\"][frames] / (beh[\"texture_length_dm\"] / 4.0)).astype(np.int64)\n            position_bin = np.clip(position_bin, 0, 3)\n            trial_output[2] = position_bin\n```\n\niii. As above; four equal 1 m bins over the 4 m textured corridor, as the instructions require. Only\ncorridor (`ft_CorrSpc`) frames are retained, so positions never legitimately exceed 40 dm.\n\n## 9-c. How is `output` *Position in corridor* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. Fixed, equal-width spatial thresholds at 10, 20 and 30 dm (1, 2, 3 m), with a defensive clip to\nthe range 0–3 so an exact 40.0 dm sample cannot produce a fifth bin. Labels\n`[\"0-1 m\", \"1-2 m\", \"2-3 m\", \"3-4 m\"]`.\n\nii.\n```python\n            position_bin = np.clip(position_bin, 0, 3)\n...\n        \"output_values\": [..., [\"0-1 m\", \"1-2 m\", \"2-3 m\", \"3-4 m\"], ...]\n```\n\niii. Directly from the decoder-task specification: \"Position in corridor discretized into 4\nequal-length, 1-m-long spatial bins\".\n\n## 9-d. How is `output` *Position in corridor* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. `ft_Pos` is one value per imaging frame, indexed with the same `frames` array used for the neural\ncolumns, so it is element-wise aligned and of the trial's length.\n\nii.\n```python\n            frames = frame_indices[columns]\n            position_bin = np.floor(beh[\"ft_Pos\"][frames] / (beh[\"texture_length_dm\"] / 4.0))\n```\n\niii. Frame-indexed alignment for every stream, as in 3-c.\n\n## 10-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Running speed* derived from?\n\ni. `ft_RunSpeed`, the running speed at each imaging frame.\n\nii.\n```python\n        speeds.append(beh[\"ft_RunSpeed\"][: len(mask)][mask].astype(np.float32))\n...\n            speed_bin = np.digitize(beh[\"ft_RunSpeed\"][frames], speed_edges, right=False)\n```\n\niii. Not discussed; it is the requested quantity, read directly.\n\n## 10-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Running speed*?\n\ni. A first pass over all 89 sessions concatenates `ft_RunSpeed` over exactly the frames the mask\nretains and computes the 25/50/75% quantiles of that pooled distribution — one **dataset-wide** set\nof thresholds rather than per-session ones. The run aborts if the three edges are not strictly\nincreasing. Each frame is then assigned to a bin by `np.digitize`. Resulting occupancies are\n25.000% per bin. The edges are stored in the metadata.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef global_speed_edges(behaviors: dict) -> np.ndarray:\n    \"\"\"Compute dataset-wide quartiles over exactly the retained samples.\"\"\"\n    speeds = []\n    for beh in behaviors.values():\n        mask = valid_frame_mask(beh, len(beh[\"ft\"]))\n        speeds.append(beh[\"ft_RunSpeed\"][: len(mask)][mask].astype(np.float32))\n    all_speeds = np.concatenate(speeds)\n    edges = np.quantile(all_speeds, (0.25, 0.50, 0.75))\n    if not np.all(np.diff(edges) > 0):\n        raise RuntimeError(f\"Running-speed quartiles are not distinct: {edges}\")\n    return edges.astype(np.float64)\n```\n```python\n            \"running_speed_quartile_edges\": speed_edges.tolist(),\n```\n\niii. Step 30: \"computes running-speed quartiles globally over the retained samples\". The docstring\nstresses that the quantiles are taken over \"exactly the retained samples\", so the four bins each hold\n25% of the data that is actually written. The agent measured the pooled quartiles in step 53–54\n(`[-19.17, 12.42, 25.35, 40.85, 163.49]`) before committing to the approach, confirming the edges are\ndistinct (the mask removes most of the zero-speed ties that would otherwise make equal-size\nvalue-threshold bins impossible).\n\n## 10-c. How is `output` *Running speed* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. `np.digitize(..., right=False)` against the three global edges, giving classes\n`[\"lowest 25%\", \"25-50%\", \"50-75%\", \"highest 25%\"]`. Thresholds are on *value*, not on per-session\nrank, so a given speed means the same class in every session, but individual sessions are not exactly\nbalanced.\n\nii.\n```python\n            speed_bin = np.digitize(beh[\"ft_RunSpeed\"][frames], speed_edges, right=False).astype(np.int64)\n            trial_output[3] = speed_bin\n```\n\niii. Follows the decoder-task requirement \"Running speed discretized into 4 bins, each corresponding\nto 25% of the data\", read as a property of the pooled dataset.\n\n## 10-d. How is `output` *Running speed* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Indexed with the same `frames` array used for the neural columns, so it is element-wise aligned\nand of the trial's length.\n\nii.\n```python\n            frames = frame_indices[columns]\n            speed_bin = np.digitize(beh[\"ft_RunSpeed\"][frames], speed_edges, right=False)\n```\n\niii. Frame-indexed alignment for every stream, as in 3-c.\n\n## 11. How are minor mistakes in the data, e.g. missing data, handled?\n\ni. Two classes of handling. (a) Benign truncation/cleaning: the behavior streams can be longer than\nthe imaging, so every stream is cut to `min(n_imaged_frames, len(ft), len(ft_trInd),\nlen(ft_CorrSpc), len(ft_move))`; `n_imaged_frames` itself is the minimum frame count across imaging\nplanes; frames whose `ft_trInd` is NaN, negative or ≥ `ntrials` are dropped; non-finite `LickFr`\nentries are dropped; `ft_Pos` bins are clipped to 0–3. (b) Everything else is **fail-fast**: a\nmissing behavior key, an experiment-table/spike-file mismatch, a retinotopy/neuron count mismatch, a\nnon-float32 spike array, a session with no valid frames, non-chronological trial indices, fewer than\ntwo usable trials, or non-distinct speed quartiles all raise and abort the whole conversion rather\nthan skipping the session. In practice none of these fired: all 89 sessions converted.\n\nii.\n```python\n    nframes = min(nframes, len(beh[\"ft\"]), len(beh[\"ft_trInd\"]),\n                  len(beh[\"ft_CorrSpc\"]), len(beh[\"ft_move\"]))\n    valid_trial = np.isfinite(trial) & (trial >= 0) & (trial < beh[\"ntrials\"])\n```\n```python\n        nframes = min(plane.shape[1] for plane in raw_planes)\n        lick_frame = lick_frame[np.isfinite(lick_frame)].astype(np.int64)\n```\n```python\n        if key not in behavior_file:\n            raise KeyError(f\"{key!r} is absent from {path}\")\n        if len(iarea) != raw_neuron_count:\n            raise RuntimeError(f\"Retinotopy/neural neuron mismatch in {sid}: ...\")\n```\n\niii. The consistency checks are written as hard assertions so that a silent mis-pairing of\nretinotopy, behavior and spikes cannot reach the output. The agent's progress notes (steps 33, 38,\n42) report each batch of sessions \"passed shape and retinotopy consistency checks\" — the checks were\nused as the running validation of the conversion.\n\n## 12-a. What are the most time-consuming steps of the code?\n\ni. Reading the 434 GB of `spk/*_neural_data.npy` files (89 pickled `.npy` dicts, 6–15 GB each) and\nwriting the 142 GB output pickle. Within a session, the fancy-index selection\n`plane[np.ix_(local_keep, frame_indices)]` materializes ~46k neurons × ~9k frames per session and is\nthe main CPU cost; the per-trial `np.ascontiguousarray` copy then rewrites the same data a second\ntime. What loops in the code could have been vectorized to improve efficiency?\n\ni. The per-trial loop rescans the whole session each iteration: `np.flatnonzero(trial_ids == trial)`\nis O(n_trials × n_kept_frames) where one `np.argsort`/`np.searchsorted` over `trial_ids` (already\nsorted) would give all trial boundaries in one pass. `np.isin(frames, lick_frame)` is likewise\nexecuted once per trial instead of building one per-frame lick flag per session, and\n`np.digitize(...)`/`np.floor(ft_Pos/...)` are called per trial instead of once per session and then\nsliced. Not discussed by the agent. These costs are negligible next to the 434 GB of I/O.\n\n## 12-c. (a) Benign truncation/cleaning: the behavior streams can be longer than\nthe imaging, so every stream is cut to `min(n_imaged_frames, len(ft), len(ft_trInd),\nlen(ft_CorrSpc), len(ft_move))`; `n_imaged_frames` itself is the minimum frame count across imaging\nplanes; frames whose `ft_trInd` is NaN, negative or ≥ `ntrials` are dropped; non-finite `LickFr`\nentries are dropped; `ft_Pos` bins are clipped to 0–3. What loops in the code could have been vectorized to improve efficiency?\n\ni. The\nAI builds a per-session frame mask = *valid trial index* AND `ft_CorrSpc` (inside the 4 m textured\ncorridor) AND `ft_move > 0` (virtual reality moving, i.e. the mouse running), then groups the\nsurviving frames by their `ft_trInd` value. A trial is the set of frames that carry its index, in\nframe order; trials therefore have variable length (11–178 frames, mean 22.3). All 38,110 trials in\nthe dataset retain at least one frame. Grey-space frames and non-running frames are excluded, so the\nframes of a trial are **not necessarily consecutive in time**.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef valid_frame_mask(beh: dict, nframes: int) -> np.ndarray:\n    \"\"\"Reproduce the paper's running-and-textured-corridor frame mask.\"\"\"\n    nframes = min(nframes, len(beh[\"ft\"]), len(beh[\"ft_trInd\"]),\n                  len(beh[\"ft_CorrSpc\"]), len(beh[\"ft_move\"]))\n    trial = beh[\"ft_trInd\"][:nframes]\n    valid_trial = np.isfinite(trial) & (trial >= 0) & (trial < beh[\"ntrials\"])\n    return valid_trial & beh[\"ft_CorrSpc\"][:nframes].astype(bool) & (beh[\"ft_move\"][:nframes] > 0)\n```\n```python\n        frame_indices = np.flatnonzero(frame_mask)\n        trial_ids = beh[\"ft_trInd\"][: len(frame_mask)][frame_mask].astype(np.int64)\n        if np.any(np.diff(trial_ids) < 0):\n            raise RuntimeError(f\"Trial indices are not chronological in {sid}\")\n...\n        kept_trials = np.unique(trial_ids)\n        for trial in kept_trials:\n            columns = np.flatnonzero(trial_ids == trial)\n            frames = frame_indices[columns]\n```\n\niii. Module docstring: \"It uses the same valid-frame definition as\n`code/utils.py::Get_dprime_selective_neuron`: the animal must be in the 4 m textured corridor and the\nVR must be moving (`ft_move > 0`).\" The agent read that function in the trajectory (steps 21/41) and\nquoted the reference comment \"only use activity inside the texture area plus mouse is running (VR\nmoving)\"; `methods.txt` likewise says \"We only considered timepoints during running for analysis,\nwhich removed time periods when the task mice stopped to collect water rewards.\" Step 25: \"I'll\npreserve those original imaging frames (rather than spatially interpolating them), because this\ndecoder is explicitly time-aligned to corridor entry; timestamps will therefore retain any gaps\ncaused by pauses.\"\n\n## 1-e. How are trials filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. No trial-level quality filter is applied. Every trial that keeps at least one running-in-corridor\nframe is written out — 38,110 of 38,110. The only trial-related guards are structural and abort the\nrun rather than drop data: a session with no valid frames, a session with fewer than two usable\ntrials, non-chronological trial indices, a retinotopy/neuron-count mismatch, or a non-float32 spike\narray all raise. The agent pre-checked in step 53–54 that no session has a trial with zero frames\n(`zero_trials: 0`) and that no session falls below two trials.\n\nii.\n```python\n        if len(frame_indices) == 0:\n            raise RuntimeError(f\"No valid running corridor frames in {sid}\")\n        if np.any(np.diff(trial_ids) < 0):\n            raise RuntimeError(f\"Trial indices are not chronological in {sid}\")\n...\n        if len(session_neural) < 2:\n            raise RuntimeError(f\"Fewer than two usable trials in {sid}\")\n```\n\niii. Implicit in the design: the `ft_move > 0` mask is treated as the paper's curation step, so\nstalled periods are removed frame-by-frame instead of by discarding trials. The agent reported\n(step 42) \"the conversion ... has retained the intended full trial set—38,110 trials total are\nexpected—while filtering only non-running frames and neurons outside the four paper-defined\nvisual-area groups.\" No justification is given anywhere for keeping trials whose retained frames\nstill straddle very long pauses (16% of trials contain a within-trial gap > 5 s, 1.3% a gap > 30 s,\nthe worst spanning 1,765 s).\n\n## 2-a. What variables in the raw data is the `neural` data derived from?\n\ni. `spks` from `spk/<session_id>_neural_data.npy` — a list of `(n_neurons, n_frames)` float32\ndeconvolved traces, one per imaging plane. The area label of each neuron comes from `iarea` in\n`retinotopy/<mouse>_<date>_trans.npz`, whose length is asserted to equal the total neuron count\nacross planes.\n\nii.\n```python\n        raw_planes = np.load(spk_path, allow_pickle=True).item()[\"spks\"]\n        nframes = min(plane.shape[1] for plane in raw_planes)\n        if any(plane.dtype != np.float32 for plane in raw_planes):\n            raise TypeError(f\"Expected float32 deconvolved traces in {spk_path}\")\n        iarea = np.load(data_root / \"retinotopy\" / retino_name)[\"iarea\"]\n        raw_neuron_count = sum(plane.shape[0] for plane in raw_planes)\n        if len(iarea) != raw_neuron_count:\n            raise RuntimeError(...)\n```\n\niii. Module docstring: \"The paper's neural arrays are Suite2p non-negative deconvolved fluorescence\ntraces.\" Metadata records `\"neural_signal\": \"Suite2p non-negative deconvolved fluorescence\"`.\n\n## 2-b. How is the `neural` data processed?\n\ni. No transformation at all: no dF/F, no smoothing, no normalization, no spatial interpolation, no\nz-scoring. The retained neurons and the retained frames are selected plane by plane (to avoid\nmaterializing the whole session), the planes are concatenated in file order, and each trial's\ncolumns are copied into a contiguous `(n_neurons, n_timepoints)` float32 array. Values are kept in\nthe source dtype (float32), giving a 142 GB pickle.\n\nii.\n```python\n        selected_planes = []\n        offset = 0\n        for plane in raw_planes:\n            local_keep = np.flatnonzero(keep_neuron[offset : offset + plane.shape[0]])\n            selected_planes.append(plane[np.ix_(local_keep, frame_indices)])\n            offset += plane.shape[0]\n        selected_neural = np.concatenate(selected_planes, axis=0)\n```\n```python\n            trial_neural = np.ascontiguousarray(selected_neural[:, columns])\n```\n\niii. Module docstring: \"No additional smoothing, normalization, or spatial interpolation is applied\nhere: spatial interpolation in the paper was for position-aligned analyses, whereas the requested\ndecoder alignment is temporal (corridor entry).\" Step 30: \"It keeps the paper's four retinotopic\nvisual-area groups, removes atlas-unassigned neurons ... the resulting pickle is expected to be\nroughly 150 GB\" — the size was anticipated and accepted rather than reduced by down-casting.\n\n## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. The only neuron-level filter is the retinotopic area: a neuron is kept iff its `iarea` code is in\nV1 `{8}`, medial `{0,1,2,9}`, anterior `{3,4}` or lateral `{5,6}`. Codes `-1` (outside the atlas) and\n`7` are dropped. This keeps 4,105,393 of 4,691,034 neurons (V1 1,833,035; medial 1,108,860;\nanterior 668,180; lateral 495,318). No further selection (no activity threshold, no SNR cut) is\napplied. Frame-level curation is the running/corridor mask of 1-d.\n\nii.\n```python\nAREA_CODES = {\"V1\": (8,), \"medial\": (0, 1, 2, 9), \"anterior\": (3, 4), \"lateral\": (5, 6)}\n\ndef area_labels(iarea):\n    region = np.full(len(iarea), -1, dtype=np.int8)\n    for idx, name in enumerate(list(AREA_CODES)):\n        region[np.isin(iarea, AREA_CODES[name])] = idx\n    keep = region >= 0\n    return keep, region[keep].astype(np.int64)\n```\n\niii. Code comment: \"Exact grouping used by `code/utils.py::neu_area_ID`. Area -1 (outside the\nretinotopic atlas) and area 7 (not assigned to one of the four visual-area groups used in the paper)\nare excluded.\" Metadata repeats this. The agent checked the `iarea` histograms of several sessions\nin step 20 before fixing the grouping. Suite2p's own cell classifier had already curated the ROIs,\nso nothing further is done.\n\n## 2-d. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n\ni. Alignment is to corridor entry: the first column of a trial is the first retained frame after the\nmouse entered the 4 m textured corridor, and the last is the last retained frame before it left.\nTrials keep their natural, variable length; nothing is padded or truncated to a common window.\n`off_start = 0.0` and `off_end = None`. Because the mask also removes non-running frames, the\ncolumns of a trial are the *running* frames of that traversal and can be separated by arbitrarily\nlong real-time gaps (median max within-trial gap 0.63 s, but 16% of trials contain a gap > 5 s and\nthe largest is 1,765 s). The real elapsed time of each column is nevertheless supplied to the decoder\nas the `time_since_trial_start_s` input.\n\nii.\n```python\n            columns = np.flatnonzero(trial_ids == trial)\n            frames = frame_indices[columns]\n            times = beh[\"ft\"][frames]\n            trial_neural = np.ascontiguousarray(selected_neural[:, columns])\n```\n```python\n            \"temporal_alignment_event\": \"entry into the 4 m textured corridor\",\n            \"off_start\": 0.0,\n            \"off_end\": None,\n```\n\niii. Step 25 (quoted above) is the only explicit justification: the original imaging frames are\npreserved rather than resampled, and the agent notes that \"timestamps will therefore retain any gaps\ncaused by pauses\". The metadata field `\"frame_filter\": \"ft_CorrSpc & (ft_move > 0) with a valid\ntrial index\"` documents the consequence.\n\n## 2-e. What is the temporal resolution (time bin size) of the converted data? Is any temporal rebinning applied?\n\ni. No rebinning or resampling. One column per two-photon imaging frame; the nominal rate of 3.17 Hz\nis recorded as `time_bin_size = 1000/3.17 = 315.46 ms`, identical for every trial and session. (The\ntrue inter-frame interval varies slightly, 0.26–0.45 s, and dropped frames mean the columns are not\nuniformly spaced in time.)\n\nii.\n```python\nFRAME_RATE_HZ = 3.17\n...\n            \"time_bin_size\": 1000.0 / FRAME_RATE_HZ,\n            \"nominal_frame_rate_hz\": FRAME_RATE_HZ,\n```\n\niii. Not argued explicitly beyond the docstring's statement that no interpolation/resampling is\napplied because the requested alignment is temporal rather than positional. The imaging frame is the\nfinest resolution available and every behavioral stream (`ft_*`) is already on that same grid.\n\n## 3-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Time to sound cue* derived from?\n\ni. `SoundTime` (per-trial MATLAB datenum timestamp of the cue) and `ft` (datenum timestamp of every\nimaging frame). The frame-number variant `SoundFr` is not used.\n\nii.\n```python\n            times = beh[\"ft\"][frames]\n            time_to_sound = ((beh[\"SoundTime\"][trial] - times) * SECONDS_PER_DAY).astype(np.float32)\n```\n\niii. The agent compared the two representations in steps 21–22, printing\n`(ft[StartFr] - Trial_start_time)*86400` and the equivalent for the grey-space event and finding\nagreement to ~0.25 s (sub-frame). Using the recorded event timestamps directly avoids interpolating\na fractional frame number onto the time axis.\n\n## 3-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Time to sound cue*?\n\ni. `(SoundTime[trial] - ft[frame]) * 86400`, i.e. MATLAB datenum days converted to seconds, stored as\nfloat32 and evaluated for every retained frame of the trial. It is positive before the cue and\nnegative after it, matching the name \"time **to** sound cue\". Observed range over the dataset:\n−1763.3 s to +723.5 s (the extremes come from the trials with long stalls described in 1-e).\n\nii.\n```python\nSECONDS_PER_DAY = 86_400.0\n...\n            trial_input[0] = time_to_sound\n...\n            \"time_to_sound_sign\": \"positive before the cue; negative after the cue\",\n```\n\niii. The sign convention is documented explicitly in the metadata. No smoothing or clipping is\napplied; the agent did not comment on the extreme values.\n\n## 3-c. How is the `input` *Time to sound cue* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. It is evaluated on exactly the frames that supply the neural columns of that trial (`frames`\nderives from the same `columns` used to slice `selected_neural`), so it is element-wise aligned and\nhas the trial's length.\n\nii.\n```python\n            columns = np.flatnonzero(trial_ids == trial)\n            frames = frame_indices[columns]\n            times = beh[\"ft\"][frames]\n            trial_neural = np.ascontiguousarray(selected_neural[:, columns])\n            time_to_sound = ((beh[\"SoundTime\"][trial] - times) * SECONDS_PER_DAY)\n```\n\niii. All streams in this dataset (`ft`, `ft_*`) share the imaging-frame grid, so indexing every\nstream with the same frame list guarantees alignment.\n\n## 4-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Day of training* derived from?\n\ni. `datexp` (the recording date string) and `mname`, taken from the index entry of each session. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Position in corridor* derived from?\n\ni. `ft_Pos`, the position inside the corridor at each imaging frame, in decimeters, together with the\nper-session `Texture_Length` field (40 dm in every session) used to set the bin width.\n\nii.\n```python\n    out[\"texture_length_dm\"] = float(beh[\"Texture_Length\"])\n...\n            position_bin = np.floor(beh[\"ft_Pos\"][frames] / (beh[\"texture_length_dm\"] / 4.0)).astype(np.int64)\n```\n\niii. Position in decimeters is divided by `Texture_Length/4 = 10` dm and floored, giving an integer\n0–3; the value is computed per frame (time-varying) and stored as one row of the int64 output\nmatrix. Resulting bin occupancies 25.00 / 24.87 / 24.96 / 25.17%.\n\nii.\n```python\n            position_bin = np.floor(beh[\"ft_Pos\"][frames] / (beh[\"texture_length_dm\"] / 4.0)).astype(np.int64)\n            position_bin = np.clip(position_bin, 0, 3)\n            trial_output[2] = position_bin\n```\n\niii. How is `output` *Position in corridor* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. Fixed, equal-width spatial thresholds at 10, 20 and 30 dm (1, 2, 3 m), with a defensive clip to\nthe range 0–3 so an exact 40.0 dm sample cannot produce a fifth bin. Labels\n`[\"0-1 m\", \"1-2 m\", \"2-3 m\", \"3-4 m\"]`.\n\nii.\n```python\n            position_bin = np.clip(position_bin, 0, 3)\n...\n        \"output_values\": [..., [\"0-1 m\", \"1-2 m\", \"2-3 m\", \"3-4 m\"], ...]\n```\n\niii. Directly from the decoder-task specification: \"Position in corridor discretized into 4\nequal-length, 1-m-long spatial bins\".\n\n## 9-d. How is `output` *Position in corridor* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. `ft_Pos` is one value per imaging frame, indexed with the same `frames` array used for the neural\ncolumns, so it is element-wise aligned and of the trial's length.\n\nii.\n```python\n            frames = frame_indices[columns]\n            position_bin = np.floor(beh[\"ft_Pos\"][frames] / (beh[\"texture_length_dm\"] / 4.0))\n```\n\niii. Frame-indexed alignment for every stream, as in 3-c.\n\n## 10-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Running speed* derived from?\n\ni. `ft_RunSpeed`, the running speed at each imaging frame.\n\nii.\n```python\n        speeds.append(beh[\"ft_RunSpeed\"][: len(mask)][mask].astype(np.float32))\n...\n            speed_bin = np.digitize(beh[\"ft_RunSpeed\"][frames], speed_edges, right=False)\n```\n\niii. Not discussed; it is the requested quantity, read directly.\n\n## 10-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Running speed*?\n\ni. A first pass over all 89 sessions concatenates `ft_RunSpeed` over exactly the frames the mask\nretains and computes the 25/50/75% quantiles of that pooled distribution — one **dataset-wide** set\nof thresholds rather than per-session ones. The run aborts if the three edges are not strictly\nincreasing. Each frame is then assigned to a bin by `np.digitize`. Resulting occupancies are\n25.000% per bin. The edges are stored in the metadata.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef global_speed_edges(behaviors: dict) -> np.ndarray:\n    \"\"\"Compute dataset-wide quartiles over exactly the retained samples.\"\"\"\n    speeds = []\n    for beh in behaviors.values():\n        mask = valid_frame_mask(beh, len(beh[\"ft\"]))\n        speeds.append(beh[\"ft_RunSpeed\"][: len(mask)][mask].astype(np.float32))\n    all_speeds = np.concatenate(speeds)\n    edges = np.quantile(all_speeds, (0.25, 0.50, 0.75))\n    if not np.all(np.diff(edges) > 0):\n        raise RuntimeError(f\"Running-speed quartiles are not distinct: {edges}\")\n    return edges.astype(np.float64)\n```\n```python\n            \"running_speed_quartile_edges\": speed_edges.tolist(),\n```\n\niii. Step 30: \"computes running-speed quartiles globally over the retained samples\". The docstring\nstresses that the quantiles are taken over \"exactly the retained samples\", so the four bins each hold\n25% of the data that is actually written. The agent measured the pooled quartiles in step 53–54\n(`[-19.17, 12.42, 25.35, 40.85, 163.49]`) before committing to the approach, confirming the edges are\ndistinct (the mask removes most of the zero-speed ties that would otherwise make equal-size\nvalue-threshold bins impossible).\n\n## 10-c. How is `output` *Running speed* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. `np.digitize(..., right=False)` against the three global edges, giving classes\n`[\"lowest 25%\", \"25-50%\", \"50-75%\", \"highest 25%\"]`. (a) Benign truncation/cleaning: the behavior streams can be longer than\nthe imaging, so every stream is cut to `min(n_imaged_frames, len(ft), len(ft_trInd),\nlen(ft_CorrSpc), len(ft_move))`; `n_imaged_frames` itself is the minimum frame count across imaging\nplanes; frames whose `ft_trInd` is NaN, negative or ≥ `ntrials` are dropped; non-finite `LickFr`\nentries are dropped; `ft_Pos` bins are clipped to 0–3. What loops in the code could have been vectorized to improve efficiency?\n\ni. The per-trial loop rescans the whole session each iteration: `np.flatnonzero(trial_ids == trial)`\nis O(n_trials × n_kept_frames) where one `np.argsort`/`np.searchsorted` over `trial_ids` (already\nsorted) would give all trial boundaries in one pass. `np.isin(frames, lick_frame)` is likewise\nexecuted once per trial instead of building one per-frame lick flag per session, and\n`np.digitize(...)`/`np.floor(ft_Pos/...)` are called per trial instead of once per session and then\nsliced. This requires examining the provided paper, code, and data to understand how the data is formatted and processed, and determine how to balance this with the requirements of the new downstream analysis.\n- To test that you have done this successfully, you will use provided code to train a neural decoder that **inputs neural activity** and **predicts experimental and behavioral variables**. \n- We will assess your work in two ways. First, statistics of the converted data and decoder accuracy will be compared to those of an expert-written solution. Second, each decision you make about how to load, filter, process, align, and save the data will be compared with the decision made by an expert. A decision that differs from the expert's is acceptable if it is equally well justified; decisions that are poorly justified will be flagged as concerning or incorrect.\n\n## Reference Information\n\n- The reference **paper** \"Unsupervised pretraining in biological neural networks\" is in the file `/app/paper.pdf`.\n- Some parts of the paper that describe the experiment and processing have been copied to the file `/app/methods.txt`. \n- **Code** from the paper are in the directory `/app/code`.\n- **Data** from the paper are in the directory `/app/data`.\n\n## Decoder Task\n\nTemporally aligned based on trial start (corridor entry).\n\n### Decoder Inputs:\n- Time to sound cue, continuous, time-varying\n- Day of training, continuous, per-trial\n- Time since trial start, continuous, time varying\n- Reward availability: 1 if in rewarded corridor, 0 if not, discrete, per-trial\n\n### Decoder Outputs\n- Visual stimulus category. e.g. circle, leaf, etc., per-trial\n- Licking, binary, time-varying. 0 = not licking, 1 = licking\n- Position in corridor discretized into 4 equal-length, 1-m-long spatial bins, time varying\n- Running speed discretized into 4 bins, each corresponding to 25% of the data\n\n## Target Data Format\n\n- Save the full converted dataset to the pickle file `/app/converted_data.pkl`.\n- All datasets must be converted into the following Python dictionary structure:\n\n```python\ndata = {\n    'neural': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # shape: (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # trial 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        [  # list of trials for session 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'input': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            input_data,  # stimulus/task variables, shape: (n_input, n_timepoints) or (n_input)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'output': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            output_data,  # behavioral response, shape (n_output, n_timepoints) or (n_output)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'subjects': list of str, # ids of all subjects, length n_subjects\n    'subject_idx': shape (n_sessions,), # index into subjects for each session\n\n    'brain_regions': list of str, # names of the brain regions recorded from, e.g. ALM, V1\n    'brain_region_idx': [ # list of sessions\n        neuron_region_idx, # shape: (n_neurons,)\n    ], # index into `brain_regions` for each neuron\n\n    'input_names': list of str, # names for each input, of length n_input\n    'output_names': list of str, # names of each output, of length n_output\n    'output_values': [ # list of length n_output\n        [ output_val0, ... ], # list of str of length d_output[0], name of each output value\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'metadata': {\n        'task_description': str, # description of output tasks\n        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\n        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\n        'off_start': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        ...\n    }\n}\n```\n\n### Key Format Requirements\n\n- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\n  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\n  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \n  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\n\n- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\n  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n\n- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n\n- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n\n- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n\n- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n\n## Decoder Reference\n\n- Run this script with `python /app/train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n- Optional arguments:\n  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n\n### Purpose\n\nThese functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n3. Values are sensible - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n4. Decoder can successfully train and predict\n5. Poor performance indicates data formatting issues that need investigation\n\n### Consistency requirements\n\nYour processing and formatting must **match the reference paper and code** with respect to:\n- Loading of data\n- Temporal alignment of different time series streams (neural, inputs, outputs)\n- Processing of neural, input, and output data streams\n- Curation of data: filtering of low-quality neurons, trials, sessions, and mice.\n\nDiscrepancies are only allowed if required by the Decoder Input and Decoder Output specifications above, or the task of training a neural decoder. During testing, a reference copy of `/app/train_decoder.py` will be run on your converted data. Data statistics and decoder accuracy will be compared to those achieved by human-written conversion code.\n\n## Success Criteria\n\nA successful conversion should:\n1. All required files must be created:\n  - `/app/convert_data.py`\n  - `/app/converted_data.pkl`\n2. Match the target data structure exactly\n3. Have consistent dimensions across trials/sessions\n4. Include all relevant data from the source\n5. Match information provided in the reference texts, code, and these instructions\n6. Outputs must match expert-written conversion code in:\n  - Dataset size and distribution statistics\n  - Decoder accuracy\n7. Each decision about how to load, filter, process, align, and save the data will be compared with the decision made by an expert. Decisions do not need to match the expert's: a different decision that is equally well justified is acceptable, while poorly justified decisions will be flagged as concerning or incorrect. Your code must implement the decisions as you describe them.\n# Decisions\n\n## 1-a. How are **all the data** for all subjects, sessions, and trials loaded in?\n\ni. Everything is read from three directories under `data`: `beh` for the behavior, `spk` for the deconvolved calcium traces and `retinotopy` for the visual area of each neuron. `beh/Imaging_Exp_info.npy` is the master index and is read first, listing every recording grouped by experiment type. A recording is one mouse on one date in one block, and that triple is the session id, which also names the spike file. Each `Beh_<exp_type>.npy` holds the behavior of several sessions keyed by that id, so the conversion loops over behavior files and reads each one once; the spikes and the retinotopy are read per session.\n\nii. The index, and the session id built from an entry of it:\n```python\nexp_info = np.load(os.path.join(BEH, 'Imaging_Exp_info.npy'), allow_pickle=True).item()\nsession_id = '%s_%s_%s' % (entry['mname'], entry['datexp'], entry['blk'])\n```\n\nThe three reads a session needs:\n```python\nbehavior = np.load(os.path.join(BEH, beh_file), allow_pickle=True).item()\nplanes = np.load(os.path.join(SPK, '%s_neural_data.npy' % session_id), allow_pickle=True).item()\narea = np.load(os.path.join(RETINO, '%s_%s_%s_%s_trans.npz' % (mouse, year, month, day)))['iarea']\n```\n\niii. The index is reorganized from experiment type into subject and session, one record per recording, which is the form the conversion iterates over.\n\n## 1-b. How are the data split into subjects?\n\ni. The mouse name is taken from `mname` in the index entry and carried on every record, and the records are grouped by it. At assembly the subjects are the sorted unique names, and `subject_idx` holds each session's index into that list: 89 sessions over 19 mice.\n\nii. The mouse of a record, and the subject list built from it:\n```python\nrecords.append((entry['mname'], session_id, 'Beh_%s.npy' % exp_type, beh_key))\n```\n```python\nsubjects = sorted({r['subject'] for r in results})\nsub_ix = {s: i for i, s in enumerate(subjects)}\n```\n\niii. The index already names the mouse, so no split has to be derived.\n\n## 1-c. How are the data split into sessions?\n\ni. A session is one mouse on one date in one block, which the index entry gives as `mname`, `datexp` and `blk`. The id built from the three names the spike file and keys the behavior. A recording listed under more than one experiment type is kept only the first time it is seen, leaving 89 sessions.\n\nii. The id, and the check that keeps each recording once:\n```python\nsession_id = '%s_%s_%s' % (entry['mname'], entry['datexp'], entry['blk'])\nif session_id in seen:\n    continue\nseen.add(session_id)\n```\n\niii. The recording sometimes repeat under different behavior session type. Therefore a recording that is listed under more than one experiment type is kept only the first time it is seen.\n\n## 1-d. Are the data correctly split into trials?\n\ni. The split into trials is given by the data: every neural frame carries its trial in `ft_trInd`, and the boundaries are listed as frame numbers as well, `StartFr` at corridor entry, `GrayFr` at the grey space and `EndFr` at the exit. Based on the instruction we define the trials and the sequence between entry to the end of texture (4m). The trials are taken as they are, and within each one the frames kept are every frame inside the texture, `ft_CorrSpc`. Nothing is cut from a trial: it runs to the end of its own traversal, so trials differ in length.\n\nii. The trials are the ones the data declares, `ntrials` of them, indexed by `ft_trInd`:\n```python\nframes = [trial_frames(beh, trial, n_frames) for trial in range(beh['ntrials'])]\n```\n\nand the frames of each are the ones it labels with that trial:\n```python\ninside = (beh['ft_trInd'][:n_frames] == trial) & beh['ft_CorrSpc'][:n_frames]\nreturn np.flatnonzero(inside)\n```\n\niii. Trial goes from start to either gray or end frame, as described in the paper. As neural decoders generally make use of temporally contiguous data, we do not drop frames in the middle of trials. \n\n## 1-e. How are trials filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. Two trials are dropped: one left with no frames once the behavior is cut to the frames that were imaged, and one longer than the 99th percentile of traversals across the whole dataset. Nothing else is filtered.\n\nii. The two conditions, and the limit they use:\n```python\ntrials = [trial for trial in range(beh['ntrials'])\n          if 0 < frames[trial].size <= limit]\n```\n```python\nlengths = get_all_trial_lengths(records, datadir)\nreturn np.percentile(lengths, TRIAL_LENGTH_PERCENTILE), lengths\n```\n\niii. The longest traversals are not slow runs, they are animals that stopped. A trial past the 99th percentile is 95% stationary, against 4% for a typical trial, so the distribution separates rather than shading off. The extreme case, trial 391 of `TX88_2022_07_19_1`, runs 5607 frames: the mouse enters, reaches 1.4 m of the 4 m corridor, stands there for 29 minutes and then runs out, contributing 5571 bins that all carry the same position label. Dropping such a trial whole is preferred to shortening it, which would keep a few hundred bins of a parked animal and invent an endpoint the traversal never had. The threshold is measured from the data rather than written down, over every session even when only a sample is converted, so that a sample run and a full one agree about which trials are too long; it comes out at 238.9 frames, 75 s, about ten times the median traversal, and removes 382 trials of 38110. No session loses more than 9.5% of its trials and none falls under the two needed to evaluate a decoder.\n\n## 2-a. What variables in the raw data is the final `neural` data derived from?\n\ni. From `spks` in `spk/<session_id>_neural_data.npy`, which is a list of one neurons by frames array per imaging plane, concatenated into a single array. The visual area of each neuron comes from `iarea` in `retinotopy/<mouse>_<date>_trans.npz`.\n\nii. The two reads and the concatenation:\n```python\nplanes = np.load(os.path.join(SPK, '%s_neural_data.npy' % session_id), allow_pickle=True).item()\nspikes = np.concatenate(planes['spks'], 0)\narea = np.load(os.path.join(RETINO, '%s_%s_%s_%s_trans.npz' % (mouse, year, month, day)))['iarea']\n```\n\niii. The neural data is read directly, nothing is derived from it.\n\n## 2-b. How is the `neural` data processed?\n\ni. The traces are not processed. The columns belonging to a trial are taken and the result is stored as float16. The trials are variable length and are left that way, so nothing is padded.\n\nii. One trial of neural data:\n```python\nneural.append(spikes[:, window].astype(np.float16))\n```\n\niii. The file already holds deconvolved traces, which the methods state every analysis was based on, so no dF/F or deconvolution is needed. float16 rather than float32 because the dataset is quite big. \n\n## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. The only filter is the visual area. A neuron is kept if its `iarea` falls in V1, mHV, lHV or aHV, and dropped otherwise, which keeps 4,105,393 of 4,691,034 neurons.\n\nii. The area of each neuron, and the neurons kept:\n```python\nregion = np.full(area.size, -1)\nfor index, name in enumerate(BRAIN_REGIONS):\n    region[np.isin(area, AREAS[name])] = index\n\nkeep = region >= 0\nreturn spikes[keep], region[keep]\n```\n\niii. The authors already curated the neurons with the Suite2p cell classifier, and the reference code applies no further filter, so nothing is done beyond assigning the area.\n\n## 2-d. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n\ni. The alignment event is corridor entry. Trials are of variable length, because mice run at different speeds, and they are stored at that length: every trial starts at its own corridor entry and ends where its traversal ends. Nothing is cut and nothing is padded.\n\nii. Every frame of the traversal, from corridor entry, and the trial built from exactly those frames:\n```python\ninside = (beh['ft_trInd'][:n_frames] == trial) & beh['ft_CorrSpc'][:n_frames]\nreturn np.flatnonzero(inside)\n```\n```python\nneural.append(spikes[:, window].astype(np.float16))\n```\n\niii. The format does not need one length for every trial. It asks that the bin *size* be the same across trials and sessions, which it is at 315 ms, and it accepts `None` for `off_end` when the trials share no common end. The decoder reads each trial's length off its own array and treats every bin as an independent sample, so a shared window buys nothing. A fixed window costs a great deal: at 32 frames it discarded 435407 real frames, 31.7% of everything inside a corridor, and replaced them with 281757 padding bins, 23.1% of what was written. Trials keep their own length instead, and the small number that are too long to be a traversal at all are dropped whole, as described in 1-e.\n\n## 2-e. How is the `neural` data temporally binned/resampled?\n\ni. It is not binned or resampled. The imaging frames are the bins: one column of `spks` per frame at 3.17 Hz, so a bin is 315 ms, and a trial holds as many of them as its traversal lasted, a median of 23.\n\nii. The rate, and the bin size it gives the metadata:\n```python\nFRAME_RATE = 3.17                # imaging rate, from the reference\n```\n```python\n'time_bin_size': 1000.0 / FRAME_RATE,        # ms\n```\n\niii. The imaging frame is the finest resolution the data has, and every behavior stream is already on the same grid, so there is nothing to resample.\n\n## 3-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *time_to_sound_cue* derived from?\n\ni. From `SoundFr`, the frame at which the sound cue was played in each trial, and `ft`, the timestamp of every imaging frame.\n\nii. The frame times of the session, and the cue of every trial placed on them:\n```python\nframe_time = (beh['ft'][:n_frames] - beh['ft'][0]) * SEC_PER_DAY\ncue = np.interp(beh['SoundFr'], index, frame_time)\n```\n\niii. The timing is dervied from the frame number.\n\n## 3-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *time_to_sound_cue*?\n\ni. The cue frame is interpolated onto the frame time axis, and the input is the cue time minus the time of each bin, in seconds.  Note that here the instruction as *time_to_sound_cue*, so it's positive before the sound cue, and negative after the sound cue.\n\nii. The time axis of the trial, and the input built from it:\n```python\nframe_time = (beh['ft'][:n_frames] - beh['ft'][0]) * SEC_PER_DAY\ncue = np.interp(beh['SoundFr'], index, frame_time)\n```\n\n```python\ntime = frame_time[window]\n```\n```python\ncue[trial] - time\n```\n\niii. The time need to be converted to seconds and compute a time difference to the timestamp of each frame.\n\n## 3-c. How is `input` *time_to_sound_cue* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. It is computed from the frame times of the same `window` used for the neural columns of that trial.\n\nii. The same frames as the neural data of the trial:\n```python\ntime = frame_time[window]\n```\n\niii. All data stream is aligned in this dataset based on frame number. \n\n## 4-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *day_of_training* derived from?\n\ni. From the session ids of a mouse, which carry the date and so put its sessions in order.\n\nii. The sessions of a mouse taken in order:\n```python\nfor subject, session_id, _, _ in sorted(records):\n```\n\n```python\ndef training_days(records):\n    \"\"\"How many days into training each session is, counted per mouse.\"\"\"\n    days, count = {}, {}\n\n    # a session id sorts by date within a mouse, so the sessions are counted in order\n    for subject, session_id, _, _ in sorted(records):\n        days[session_id] = count.get(subject, 0)\n        count[subject] = days[session_id] + 1\n\n    return days\n```\n\niii. The date string is the only field that orders every session of every mouse, so it is used to put them in order, and the *day_of_training* variable is computed from how many of the training session come before.\n\n## 4-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *day_of_training*?\n\ni. The day is how many recorded days the mouse is into training, so its first session is 0 and each later one counts up, to at most 7. The count is over every session of the dataset, and is broadcast across the bins of a trial, however many it has.\n\nii. The count, and the broadcast:\n```python\ndef training_days(records):\n    \"\"\"How many days into training each session is, counted per mouse.\"\"\"\n    days, count = {}, {}\n\n    # a session id sorts by date within a mouse, so the sessions are counted in order\n    for subject, session_id, _, _ in sorted(records):\n        days[session_id] = count.get(subject, 0)\n        count[subject] = days[session_id] + 1\n\n    return days\n```\n\niii. The recording days of a mouse are not consecutive, counting the days it was recorded on gives how far into training it is.\n\n## 5-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *time_since_trial_start* derived from?\n\ni. From `StartFr`, the frame at which the mouse entered the corridor on each trial, and `ft`, the timestamp of every imaging frame.\n\nii. The corridor entry of every trial placed on the frame times:\n```python\nstart = np.interp(beh['StartFr'], index, frame_time)\n```\n\niii. N/A\n\n## 5-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *time_since_trial_start*?\n\ni. The entry frame is fractional, so it is interpolated onto the frame time axis, and the input is the time of each bin minus that entry, in seconds, starting near zero. Note that this is *time_since_trial_start* so it is negative before start, postive after.\n\nii. The input built from the trial time axis:\n```python\ntime - start[trial]\n```\n\niii. Simply compute a time difference to the timestamp of each frame.\n\n## 5-c. How is `input` *time_since_trial_start* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. It is computed from the frame times of the same `window` used for the neural columns of that trial.\n\nii. The same frames as the neural data of the trial:\n```python\ntime = frame_time[window]\n```\n\niii. All data stream is aligned in this dataset based on frame number. \n\n## 6-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *reward_availability* derived from?\n\ni. From `isRew`, which marks the trials run in the rewarded corridor.\n\nii. The flag of every trial:\n```python\nreward = beh['isRew'].astype(int)\n```\n\n\"\"\"Convert the Zhong et al. 2025 virtual reality dataset into the decoder format.\n\nUsage: python -u convert_data.py <outpicklefile> [--full | --sample] [--datadir DIR]\n\"\"\"\n\nimport argparse\nimport os\nimport pickle\nimport time\n\nimport numpy as np\nfrom tqdm import tqdm\n\n_zhong2025_seed = 'x5cidj2hy87s'\nnp.random.seed(sum(ord(c) for c in _zhong2025_seed) % 2**31)\n\n# --- constants ---\n\nHERE = os.path.dirname(os.path.abspath(__file__))\n# Directory holding the three data subfolders. Harbor mounts the dataset at /app/data\n# while this script runs from /solution, so solve.sh overrides this with --datadir.\n#   beh/         behavior, one file per experiment type\n#   spk/         deconvolved traces, one file per session\n#   retinotopy/  visual area of each neuron\nDATA_DIR = os.path.join(HERE, 'data')\n\nSEC_PER_DAY = 24 * 60 * 60       # the times are MATLAB datenums, so in days\nFRAME_RATE = 3.17                # imaging rate, from the reference notebook\n\n# Some animals sit still for a really long time in the middle of a trial\n# Median trial length is 23 frames, but
